We construct this modified filtration below, including its initial page and convergence. Throughout this section, cohomological degree and weight are denoted by $(s,w)$. Unless an Adams–Novikov differential is named explicitly, the spectral sequences in this section are algebraic spectral sequences of cobar complexes. Put $$P=\mathbb F_p[t_1,t_2,\ldots],\qquad
 |t_i|_w=u_i,
 \qquad
 \overline\Delta(t_i)=\sum_{0<l<i}t_l\otimes t_{i-l}^{p^l}.$$ Reversing all coproducts reverses the cobar conventions and changes none of the conclusions. We use the displayed convention.

<a id="stmt-may-page"></a>
<div id="may:page" class="lemma">

<a id="may:initial-page"></a>
**Lemma 10** (The filtered cobar calculation). *For every integer $M\geq0$, the cobar cohomology of $P$ admits a multiplicative, degreewise finite spectral sequence whose initial cohomology page is $$\Lambda(h_{i,j}:i\geq1,j\geq0)
 \otimes\mathbb F_p[b_{i,j}:i\geq1,j\geq0].
 \label{may:initial-page}$$ The bidegrees and auxiliary filtrations are $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 & (s,w)& F_M\\ \hline
 h_{i,j}&(1,p^ju_i)&2i-1+M[j=0]\\
 b_{i,j}&(2,p^{j+1}u_i)&p(2i-1+M[j=0]).
\end{array}$$ Here $[j=0]$ is $1$ or $0$ according as $j=0$ or $j\neq0$. All differentials lower $F_M$. If an exterior generator is written $h_{[a,b)}=h_{b-a,a}$, its first differential is <a id="may:splitting"></a>$$d_1 h_{[a,b)}=\sum_{a<l<b}h_{[a,l)}h_{[l,b)},
 \qquad d_1b_{i,j}=0.
 \label{may:splitting}$$ The same statements hold for the Hopf subalgebra $P_r=\mathbb F_p[t_1,\ldots,t_r]$, with $i\leq r$ in <a href="#may:initial-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:initial-page">[may:initial-page]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-page"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-page</summary>

*Proof.* Write every exponent in base $p$ and assign a digit factor $t_i^{p^j}$ the filtration $2i-1+M[j=0]$. For a monomial, add these filtrations with the digit multiplicities, without carrying. Carrying $p$ copies of one digit to the next digit lowers filtration: its decrease is $$p(2i-1+M[j=0])-(2i-1+M[j+1=0])\geq p-1>1.$$ A nonprimitive coproduct summand of $t_i^{p^j}$ replaces it by $t_l^{p^j}\otimes t_{i-l}^{p^{j+l}}$ and decreases filtration by exactly one. These observations show that this is a multiplicative coalgebra filtration, and that its associated graded Hopf algebra is the tensor product of primitive truncated polynomial Hopf algebras $$\bigotimes_{i,j}\mathbb F_p[z_{i,j}]/(z_{i,j}^p).$$ For one such factor the dual algebra is again a truncated polynomial algebra: factorial rescaling identifies its basis in exponents less than $p$ with the ordinary polynomial basis. The free resolution alternating multiplication by its generator and its $(p-1)$st power computes its cohomology as one exterior generator in degree one and one polynomial generator in degree two. Their weights are respectively the weight of $z_{i,j}$ and $p$ times that weight. Tensoring these resolutions gives <a href="#may:initial-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:initial-page">[may:initial-page]</a>, including the stated auxiliary filtrations. The exterior square is zero since its cohomological degree is odd and $p$ is odd.

The filtration-one part of the reduced coproduct gives the splitting formula for $h$. For the polynomial generator associated to a digit $z$, a filtration-one contribution would replace one of its $p$ copies by a split pair and leave $p-1$ unsplit copies of $z$. In the associated-graded cohomology of the $z$ factor, a nonzero multiplicity has the form $pa+\epsilon$, with $a\geq0$ and $\epsilon\in\{0,1\}$. The multiplicity $p-1$ has neither form, so that component has zero cohomology. Carries cannot contribute to this first differential by the strict inequality above. This proves $d_1b=0$.

In a fixed positive internal weight only finitely many digit factors occur, every monomial exponent is bounded, and a normalized bar has positive weight. Its length is consequently bounded as well. Thus all the filtrations under discussion are finite in each weight, and their spectral sequences converge to the indicated cobar cohomology. The construction restricts to $P_r$ because its coproduct uses only the first $r$ coordinates. ◻

</details>

<a id="may:low-alphabet"></a>
<a id="may:three-position-weight"></a>
For later calculations we fix the following notation, always distinguishing the bare exterior letter $b$ from the indexed polynomial letters: $$\begin{gathered}
 x=h_{1,0},\quad a=h_{2,0},\quad c=h_{3,0},\qquad
 y=h_{1,1},\quad b=h_{2,1},\quad z=h_{1,2},\\
 A=b_{1,0},\qquad B=b_{2,0},\qquad C=b_{1,1}.
\end{gathered}
\label{may:low-alphabet}$$ An exterior interval $h_{i,j}$ covers positions $j,\ldots,j+i-1$ in its weight, and a polynomial letter $b_{i,j}$ covers positions $j+1,\ldots,j+i$. Let $m$ be the number of exterior letters, let $H_r$ count those covering position $r$, and let $L=(s-m)/2$ be the number of polynomial letters, counted with multiplicity. If only positions $0,1,2$ occur, the alphabet is precisely <a href="#may:low-alphabet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:low-alphabet">[may:low-alphabet]</a>. Writing $n_A,n_B,n_C$ for the polynomial multiplicities gives $$w=H_0+p(H_1+L-n_C)+p^2(H_2+n_B+n_C),
 \qquad n_A=L-n_B-n_C.
 \label{may:three-position-weight}$$

<a id="low:section"></a>
### The unital complexes through height three

We first construct the lower complexes, retaining a binary two-sided unit. The algebraic input is the May upper bound of Lemma <a href="#may:page" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:page">10</a>: a bidegree containing no initial-page monomial has zero cobar cohomology. The two vanishing families below serve different parts of the induction: the first makes each $v_n$ survive to a self-map, and the second permits the two unit maps to extend to a binary product over every pair of cells.

<a id="stmt-low-algebraic-vanishing"></a>
<div id="low:algebraic-vanishing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Lower obstruction groups). *The following cobar cohomology groups vanish.*

1.  *For $1\leq n\leq3$ and $k\geq1$, the group with coefficients $R/I_n$ in bidegree $$(s,w)=(qk+1,u_n+k).$$*

2.  *For $0\leq n\leq3$, choose $e_i\in\{0,1,2\}$ for $0\leq i\leq n$ and put $$U=\sum_{i=0}^n e_i u_i,\qquad
      \rho=\sum_{i=0}^n e_i\geq2.$$ For every $k\geq1$, the group with coefficients $R/I_{n+1}$ in bidegree $$(s,w)=(qk+1-\rho,U+k)$$ is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-low-algebraic-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-low-algebraic-vanishing</summary>

<a id="low:normalized-cutoff"></a>
*Proof.* Include the first case in the notation of the second by setting $U=u_n$ and $\rho=0$, while retaining its indicated coefficient comodule. If the normalized cochain group in degree $(s,w)$ is nonzero, Lemma <a href="#fw:normalized-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:normalized-bound">4</a> gives $$2015k\leq U+\rho-1.
 \label{low:normalized-cutoff}$$ The exact values $$(u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3,u_4)=(0,1,1010,1{,}019{,}091,1{,}028{,}262{,}820)$$ give Table <a href="#low:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:cutoffs">1</a>. Each upper bound is obtained by substituting the displayed maxima in Equation <a href="#low:normalized-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:normalized-cutoff">[low:normalized-cutoff]</a> and taking the integer part. A zero upper bound on $k$ excludes all $k\geq1$.

<div id="low:cutoffs">

| Case           |     Maximum $U$ | Maximum $\rho$ | Maximum $k$ |     Maximum $w$ |
|:---------------|----------------:|---------------:|------------:|----------------:|
| Product, $n=0$ |             $0$ |            $2$ |         $0$ |               — |
| Product, $n=1$ |             $2$ |            $4$ |         $0$ |               — |
| Product, $n=2$ |          $2022$ |            $6$ |         $1$ |          $2023$ |
| Product, $n=3$ | $2{,}040{,}204$ |            $8$ |      $1012$ | $2{,}041{,}216$ |
| $v_1$ target   |             $1$ |            $0$ |         $0$ |               — |
| $v_2$ target   |          $1010$ |            $0$ |         $0$ |               — |
| $v_3$ target   | $1{,}019{,}091$ |            $0$ |       $505$ | $1{,}019{,}596$ |

Preliminary bounds for all lower obstruction bidegrees.

</div>

These bounds also justify the additive comparison in the adjacent cochain degrees. For either remaining product case, $w<u_{n+1}$, so no positive coefficient or coordinate $t_i$ with $i\geq n+1$ can occur. The retained coproducts are additive modulo $I_{n+1}$, and the last assertion of Lemma <a href="#fw:range-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:range-comparison">5</a> applies in every degree. For a remaining $v_n$ target, a positive coefficient costs at least $u_n$, leaving at most $k$ weight for $d\geq s-1=qk>k$ reduced bars when $d\in\{s-1,s,s+1\}$. Such a coefficient cannot occur. A bar containing $t_n$ or a higher coordinate leaves at most $k$ for the other $d-1\geq qk-1>k$ bars and cannot occur either. Hence the same three cochain degrees are additive. In all cases, both adjacent differentials are identified, so the desired cohomology is identified with that for $P$.

Every remaining weight in Table <a href="#low:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:cutoffs">1</a> is at most $2{,}041{,}216$, whereas $$p^3=1{,}027{,}243{,}729.$$ A May letter covering position $3$ or above has weight at least $p^3$. Thus only positions $0,1,2$ can occur. We use the common exterior and polynomial alphabet of Equation <a href="#may:low-alphabet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:low-alphabet">[may:low-alphabet]</a>, with the interval coverage conventions of Section <a href="#may:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:preliminaries">3</a>. Allowing this entire alphabet only enlarges the upper bound if fewer coordinates were retained in the preceding comparison.

Suppose a monomial of the desired bidegree exists. Let $m\leq6$ be its number of exterior letters and $L=(s-m)/2$ its total polynomial exponent. Every polynomial letter has weight at least $p$, so $$U+k\geq pL=p(p-1)k+\frac{p(1-\rho-m)}2.$$ For all profiles under consideration, $U\leq2p^2+4p+6$ and $\rho\leq8$. Consequently <a id="low:polynomial-cutoff"></a>$$(p^2-p-1)k
 \leq U+\frac{p(\rho+5)}2
 \leq 2p^2+\frac{21}2p+6.
 \label{low:polynomial-cutoff}$$ If $k\geq3$, the left side exceeds the last expression by at least $$p^2-\frac{27}2p-9=\frac{2{,}008{,}901}{2}>0.$$ It follows that $1\leq k\leq2$.

<a id="low:digits"></a>
<a id="low:weight-equation"></a>
Write $U=F_0+pF_1+p^2F_2$. In a product profile the digits are $$F_0=e_1+e_2+e_3,\qquad F_1=e_2+e_3,\qquad F_2=e_3,$$ with missing $e_i$ set to zero. For a $v_n$ target they are initial strings of ones. In every case, $$6\geq F_0\geq F_1\geq F_2\geq0,
 \qquad F_1\leq4,\quad F_2\leq2.
 \label{low:digits}$$ Let $H_r$ count the exterior letters covering position $r$, and let $n_B,n_C$ be the polynomial exponents of $B,C$. Adding weights gives the exact identity $$F_0+k+pF_1+p^2F_2
  =H_0+p(H_1+L-n_C)+p^2(H_2+n_B+n_C).
 \label{low:weight-equation}$$ We next extract its digits without an unproved no-carry assumption.

First, $0\leq H_0\leq3$ and $1\leq F_0+k\leq8<p$. Reduction modulo $p$ therefore gives $H_0=F_0+k$. Also $B,C$ each cost at least $p^2$, so $$p^2(n_B+n_C)\leq w\leq2p^2+4p+8<3p^2;$$ the last strict inequality has difference $p^2-4p-8=1{,}014{,}037>0$. Hence $n_B+n_C\leq2$.

<a id="low:coverage-relations"></a>
Put $\delta=(m+\rho-1)/2$. It is an integer because $s-m$ is even. For a target with $\rho=0$, that parity forces $m$ odd, hence $m\geq1$; for a product profile $\rho\geq2$. Thus $\delta\geq0$. The bounds $m\leq6$, $\rho\leq8$ and the same parity give $\delta\leq6$. Now $L=(p-1)k-\delta$. Subtracting the position-zero equation from Equation <a href="#low:weight-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:weight-equation">[low:weight-equation]</a>, dividing by $p$, and rearranging gives $$H_1-k-\delta-n_C-F_1
   =p(F_2-k-H_2-n_B-n_C).$$ Since $H_1\leq4$, the left side lies in $[-14,3]$. Its only possible multiple of $p=1009$ is zero. We have therefore proved the three exact relations $$H_0=F_0+k,\qquad
 H_2+n_B+n_C=F_2-k,\qquad
 H_1=F_1+k+\delta+n_C.
 \label{low:coverage-relations}$$

Among the exterior letters covering position zero, only $x,a$ fail to cover position two. Thus $H_2\geq H_0-2$. Equations <a href="#low:digits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:digits">[low:digits]</a> and <a href="#low:coverage-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:coverage-relations">[low:coverage-relations]</a> imply $$F_0-F_2+2k\leq2.$$ As $k\geq1$, equality is forced: $k=1$ and $F_0=F_1=F_2=f$. All the intervening inequalities are equalities, giving $$n_B=n_C=0,\qquad H_0=f+1,\qquad H_2=f-1.$$ The bounds on $H_0,H_2$ leave only $f=1,2$.

If $f=1$, the exterior letters $x,a$ must occur and $c,b,z$ must be absent. Only $y$ is optional, so $H_1\leq2$ and $m\geq2$. On the other hand, the last relation in Equation <a href="#low:coverage-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:coverage-relations">[low:coverage-relations]</a> gives $H_1=2+\delta>2$, since $\delta=(m+\rho-1)/2>0$. If $f=2$, the letters $x,a,c$ must occur and $b,z$ must be absent. Again only $y$ is optional, so $H_1\leq3$ and $m\geq3$, whereas the same relation gives $H_1=3+\delta>3$. Both possibilities are impossible. There is no monomial on the May upper-bound page, and hence no cobar cohomology in any of the stated bidegrees. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-low-finite-localization"></a>
<div id="low:finite-localization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Finite local cell attachments). *A spectrum obtained from finitely many $p$-local sphere cells is the $p$-localization of a finite CW spectrum.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-low-finite-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-low-finite-localization</summary>

*Proof.* Proceed one attachment at a time. If the previously constructed spectrum is the localization of a finite spectrum $Z$, an attaching map belongs to $\pi_d(Z_{(p)})=\pi_d(Z)\otimes\mathbb Z_{(p)}$ and can be written $a/m$ with $p\nmid m$. After localization, multiplication by $m$ on its sphere source is an equivalence. The localized cone of the integral attaching map $a$ is therefore equivalent to the cone of $a/m$. Induction over the finitely many cells proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-low-v3"></a>
<div id="low:V3" class="proposition">

<a id="low:cell-dimensions"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Construction through $V(3)$). *For $0\leq n\leq3$ there is a finite $p$-local cell spectrum $V(n)$ with a bottom-cell unit $\eta_n:S_{(p)}\to V(n)$ and a binary multiplication $$\mu_n:V(n)\wedge V(n)\longrightarrow V(n)$$ whose two unit identities hold. Its cells are indexed by subsets of $\{0,\ldots,n\}$, in dimensions $$\sum_{i\in S}(qu_i+1),
 \label{low:cell-dimensions}$$ and its $BP$-homology is the canonical quotient comodule $R/I_{n+1}$, generated in degree zero by the bottom unit. In particular, we may fix <a id="low:Y"></a>$$Y=V(3),\qquad BP_*Y=R/I_4,
 \label{low:Y}$$ with such a binary two-sided unital multiplication.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-low-v3"><summary>Proof of stmt-low-v3</summary>

*Proof.* Start with $V(0)$, the cofiber of $p:S_{(p)}\to S_{(p)}$. Since multiplication by $p$ is injective on $R$, its $BP$-homology is $R/(p)$, with the canonical quotient coaction and bottom generator. Its cells are in dimensions zero and one, as in Equation <a href="#low:cell-dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:cell-dimensions">[low:cell-dimensions]</a>.

We give first the product-extension argument, which applies to this initial complex and to every later complex once its asserted homology and cells have been obtained. The subcomplex $$U_n=(S_{(p)}\wedge V(n))
       \mathop{\cup}_{S_{(p)}\wedge S_{(p)}}
       (V(n)\wedge S_{(p)})
       \ \subseteq\ V(n)\wedge V(n)$$ has a map to $V(n)$ given by the two identity maps, which agree on the common bottom sphere. Every remaining cell corresponds to a pair of nonempty subsets of $\{0,\ldots,n\}$. If $e_i$ counts occurrences of $i$ in that pair, its dimension is $qU+\rho$, with precisely the parameters in the second part of Lemma <a href="#low:algebraic-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:algebraic-vanishing">11</a>. The obstruction to extending across the cell lies in $\pi_{qU+\rho-1}V(n)$.

An Adams–Novikov term in that stem has $qw-s=qU+\rho-1$, or $$w=U+k,\qquad s=qk+1-\rho.$$ Since $2\leq\rho\leq8<q$, nonnegative filtration requires $k\geq1$. Every corresponding $E_2$ group is zero by Lemma <a href="#low:algebraic-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:algebraic-vanishing">11</a>. Strong convergence and finite filtration, from Proposition <a href="#fw:anss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:anss">6</a>, give $\pi_{qU+\rho-1}V(n)=0$. Extending cell by cell therefore produces $\mu_n$, agreeing with both identity maps on $U_n$. This constructs the initial product on $V(0)$ as well.

Suppose now that $V(n-1)$, including its product, has been constructed, where $1\leq n\leq3$. By Lemma <a href="#fw:coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:coordinates">2</a>, $v_n$ is a primitive element of $BP_*V(n-1)=R/I_n$. It defines a filtration-zero $E_2$ class. Its possible positive differential targets are exactly Equation <a href="#fw:primitive-targets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fw:primitive-targets">[fw:primitive-targets]</a>; the first part of Lemma <a href="#low:algebraic-vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:algebraic-vanishing">11</a> makes them all zero. There is no incoming differential to filtration zero. The edge assertion in Proposition <a href="#fw:anss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:anss">6</a> consequently realizes $v_n$ by a homotopy map $$\widetilde v_n:S^{qu_n}_{(p)}\longrightarrow V(n-1).$$ Use the product to form $$f_n=\mu_{n-1}(\widetilde v_n\wedge\operatorname{id}):
       \Sigma^{qu_n}V(n-1)\longrightarrow V(n-1),$$ and define $V(n)$ as its cofiber.

The induced map on $BP$-homology is multiplication by $v_n$: the coefficient pairing is $R$-bilinear, the homology is cyclic on the unit, and $\widetilde v_n$ has the specified Hurewicz image. This multiplication is injective on the polynomial quotient $R/I_n$. The cofiber exact sequence therefore gives precisely $R/I_{n+1}$, with no additional shifted kernel summand. The bottom sphere map surjects onto this cyclic homology, so its kernel $I_{n+1}$ identifies the coaction with the canonical quotient coaction, as well as fixing the generator in degree zero.

The mapping cone adds the old cells shifted by $qu_n+1$, giving exactly Equation <a href="#low:cell-dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="low:cell-dimensions">[low:cell-dimensions]</a>. The product-extension argument already proved then constructs $\mu_n$. This completes the induction. All attachments are finite $p$-local cell attachments, and Lemma <a href="#low:finite-localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:finite-localization">12</a> gives finiteness in the sense of the stated realization problem. No associativity of any $\mu_n$ has been asserted or used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-low-p-null"></a>
<div id="low:p-null" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14** (The action of $p$ on $Y$). *For the spectrum $Y$ of Proposition <a href="#low:V3" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:V3">13</a>, $\pi_0Y=\mathbb F_p$ and $p\operatorname{id}_Y$ is nullhomotopic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-low-p-null"><summary>Proof of stmt-low-p-null</summary>

*Proof.* The bottom two cells form the mod-$p$ Moore spectrum. All other cells have dimension at least $qu_1+1=q+1>1$, so they do not change $\pi_0$. Thus $p\eta_3=0$ in $\pi_0Y$. Applying the left unit identity of its binary multiplication gives $$p\operatorname{id}_Y
  =\mu_3\bigl((p\eta_3)\wedge\operatorname{id}_Y\bigr)=0.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="may:section"></a>
### The height-four obstruction calculation

The algebraic spectral sequences below use the filtered cobar complexes of Section <a href="#may:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:preliminaries">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Possible differential targets

<a id="may:obstruction-degrees"></a>
We now use the finite spectrum $Y$ furnished by Proposition <a href="#low:V3" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:V3">13</a>. Its homology is $R/I_4$, its bottom cell is a unit, and it has a binary two-sided unital multiplication. The possible positive Adams–Novikov differentials from $v_4$ have targets $$s=qk+1,\qquad w=u_4+k,\qquad k\geq1.
 \label{may:obstruction-degrees}$$

<a id="stmt-may-target-bound"></a>
<div id="may:target-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Explicit cutoffs). *The groups in <a href="#may:obstruction-degrees" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:obstruction-degrees">[may:obstruction-degrees]</a> are identified with $H^{s,w}(P)$. Their May page has only positions $0,1,2,3$, and it is zero unless $k\leq p+2$. For $k\geq2$, only positions $0,1,2$ can occur.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-target-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-target-bound</summary>

*Proof.* If $w<s$, the degree-$(s,w)$ cochain groups for both $Y$ and $P$ are zero, as is the May page, and all the conclusions are immediate. We may therefore suppose $w\geq s$. The normalized bound in Lemma <a href="#fw:normalized-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:normalized-bound">4</a> then gives $(q-1)k\leq u_4-1$. Consequently $$w\leq u_4+\frac{u_4-1}{q-1}
 <\frac q{q-1}u_4<4p^3<p^4
 \quad (p=1009),$$ where $u_4<2p^3$ and $q/(q-1)<2$. Thus no letter can reach position four. Also $w<u_5$. A coefficient containing $v_4$ leaves at most $k$ weight for normalized bars, whereas the three lengths $s-1,s,s+1$ are all greater than $k$, because $s-1=qk>k$. Higher coefficient generators are excluded by $w<u_5$. The adjacent-chain comparison of Lemma <a href="#fw:additive-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:additive-comparison">9</a> therefore identifies the required cohomology with that of $P$.

There are ten possible exterior intervals on positions $0,1,2,3$. Since $s$ is odd, $m$ is odd and hence $m\leq9$. Every polynomial letter has weight at least $p$, so $$u_4+k\geq pL\geq p((p-1)k-4),
 \qquad
 (p^2-p-1)k\leq u_4+4p.$$ But $$(p^2-p-1)(p+3)-(u_4+4p)=p^2-9p-4>0
 \quad (p=1009).$$ It follows that $k\leq p+2$.

For $k\geq2$ we have $L\geq2p-6$. If any letter reaches position three, that letter has weight at least $p^3$, while all other polynomial letters have total weight at least $(L-1)p$. This is a valid lower bound whether the distinguished letter is exterior or polynomial. Thus $$w\geq p^3+2p^2-7p
 >p^3+p^2+2p+3=u_4+p+2\geq w,$$ since the strict difference is $p^2-9p-3>0$ at $p=1009$. This contradiction excludes position three in that range. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The first obstruction

<a id="may:specialized-obstruction"></a>
The first possible differential is $d_{q+1}$, and its target is the actual cobar cohomology in degree $(q+1,u_4+1)$. We first explain why an injective multiplication on this group will rule out the differential. Lemma <a href="#fw:v5-relation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:v5-relation">3</a> gives $$v_4h_{1,4}+e v_4^p h_{1,0}=0$$ in the $E_2$ algebra for $Y$. By $q$-sparsity no earlier positive differential occurs, so Proposition <a href="#fw:anss-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:anss-pairing">7</a> makes $d_{q+1}$ a derivation on this algebra. Let $\sigma$ denote the algebraic specialization to $P$-cohomology. Differentiate the relation and then apply $\sigma$. All terms containing $v_4$ specialize to zero, and $d_{q+1}(v_4^p)=p v_4^{p-1}d_{q+1}(v_4)=0$. Hence $$\sigma\bigl(d_{q+1}(v_4)\bigr)h_{1,4}=0.
 \label{may:specialized-obstruction}$$ Specialization is applied after the topological differential, to an identity of cobar-cohomology classes; no spectrum realizing $\sigma$ is required.

It remains to prove that multiplication by $h_{1,4}$ is injective on $H^{q+1,u_4+1}(P)$. Its degree shift is $(1,p^4)$, so its product lies in degree $(q+2,u_5+1)$. An incoming May differential to that product must have source degree $(q+1,u_5+1)$. The next lemma lists the initial May monomials in the obstruction degree and in this incoming-source degree. We will use the digit-zero adjustment of Lemma <a href="#may:page" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:page">10</a> to separate the surviving source filtrations from the product filtration.

<a id="may:small-splitting"></a>
In the alphabet of Equation <a href="#may:low-alphabet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:low-alphabet">[may:low-alphabet]</a>, the splitting differential is $$da=xy,\qquad db=yz,\qquad dc=xb+az,
 \label{may:small-splitting}$$ with the exterior order $x,a,c,y,b,z$ used for signs.

<a id="stmt-may-first-list"></a>
<div id="may:first-list" class="lemma">

<a id="may:first-target-list"></a>
**Lemma 16** (The first target and all incoming-source intervals). *In bidegree $(s,w)=(q+1,u_4+1)$ the complete initial May page consists of $$acb B^{p-2},\qquad acybz B^{p-3}.
 \label{may:first-target-list}$$ The first is not a splitting cycle and the second is a splitting cycle. In bidegree $(q+1,u_5+1)$ the complete list is given in Table <a href="#may:n5-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:n5-table">2</a>. The four entries of exterior length three or five have no nonzero splitting cycle in their span.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-first-list"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-first-list</summary>

<a id="may:L-range"></a>
*Proof.* We prove exhaustion simultaneously for $w=u_n+1$, with $n=4,5$. Since $u_n+1<p^n$, the available exterior intervals are $[a,b)$ with $0\leq a<b\leq n$, and the polynomial intervals cover positions $1,\ldots,n-1$. The target weight has base-$p$ digits $(2,1,\ldots,1)$. At position zero, $H_0\leq n<p$, so $H_0=2$ and there is no carry into position one. As $s=2p-1$ is odd, write $$m=2D-1,\qquad L=p-D.$$ There are exactly two exterior intervals starting at zero; at most $n(n-1)/2$ others are available. Hence $m\leq11$, $2\leq D\leq6$, and $$p-6\leq L\leq p-2.
 \label{may:L-range}$$ The lower bound on $D$ follows from $m$ being odd and at least two.

<a id="may:carry-equations"></a>
Let $c_r$ be the carry into position $r$, with $c_1=c_n=0$, and let $B_r$ count polynomial intervals covering position $r$. The largest possible exterior coverage at a position is nine. Inductively, the quantity $H_r+B_r+c_r$ is at most $9+(p-2)+1=p+8<2p$; therefore every carry is zero or one. The digit equations are $$B_r=1-H_r-c_r+pc_{r+1},\qquad 1\leq r\leq n-1.
 \label{may:carry-equations}$$ If there were no carry, then $B_r\leq1$ for every $r$, and $L\leq\sum B_r\leq4$, contrary to $L\geq p-6=1003$.

<a id="may:gap-after-block"></a>
Call a position high when $c_{r+1}=1$. A high position has $B_r\geq p-9$, and a nonhigh position has $B_r\leq1$. There cannot be two blocks of high positions. Indeed, choose high positions $a<b$ in different blocks and a nonhigh position $c$ between them. An interval covering both $a$ and $b$ also covers $c$, whence $$L\geq B_a+B_b-B_c\geq2(p-9)-1=2p-19>p-2.$$ Here $2p-19=1999$ and $p-2=1007$. This contradicts <a href="#may:L-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:L-range">[may:L-range]</a>. Thus the high positions form a single block $[l,r]$ with $1\leq l\leq r\leq n-2$. At the next position <a href="#may:carry-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:carry-equations">[may:carry-equations]</a> reads $B_{r+1}=-H_{r+1}$, so $$B_{r+1}=H_{r+1}=0.
 \label{may:gap-after-block}$$ At the beginning of the block, $B_l=p+1-H_l\leq L$, giving $H_l\geq D+1$. If $l<r$, its end gives $B_r=p-H_r\leq L$, hence $H_r\geq D$.

A singleton block is impossible. Every exterior interval covering its position $l$ must end at $l+1$ by <a href="#may:gap-after-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:gap-after-block">[may:gap-after-block]</a>. When $l=1$ there are only two such intervals. When $l>1$, at most one begins before $l$, since $H_{l-1}\leq1$, and at most one begins at $l$. Thus in either case $H_l\leq2$, contradicting $H_l\geq D+1\geq3$. We henceforth have $l<r$.

<a id="may:forced-profile"></a>
If $l>1$, at most two exterior intervals cover both $l$ and $r$: they must end at $r+1$, and at most one starts before $l$, while at most one starts at $l$. At least one of the two intervals starting at zero misses both positions, since $H_{l-1}\leq1$. Consequently $$H_l+H_r
 =m+\#\{\text{intervals meeting both}\}
       -\#\{\text{intervals meeting neither}\}
 \leq m+1.$$ This contradicts $H_l+H_r\geq2D+1=m+2$. Therefore $l=1$. There are now at most two exterior intervals covering both endpoints, namely $[0,r+1)$ and $[1,r+1)$. Equality in the same counting identity is forced: both are present, every exterior interval meets position $1$ or position $r$, and $$H_1=D+1,\qquad H_r=D.$$ It follows that $B_1=B_r=L$. Every polynomial interval therefore covers both positions, and by <a href="#may:gap-after-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:gap-after-block">[may:gap-after-block]</a> every one is exactly $[1,r+1)$. No exterior or polynomial interval can occur after the block. The carry supplies the target digit at $r+1$; all further digits would be zero. Since the required remaining digits are all one, necessarily $r=n-2$. Thus $$\text{polynomial factor }b_{n-2,0}^{p-D},\qquad
 H_0=2,\quad H_1=D+1,\quad
 H_2=\cdots=H_{n-2}=D,
 \quad H_{n-1}=0.
 \label{may:forced-profile}$$

It remains to list a small, now forced set of exterior intervals. For $n=4$, the intervals $[0,3)$ and $[1,3)$ are mandatory. The other start-zero interval must be $[0,2)$; $[0,1)$ would meet neither endpoint. The only optional intervals are $[1,2)$ and $[2,3)$. The coverage requirements in <a href="#may:forced-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:forced-profile">[may:forced-profile]</a> require both or neither. This is exactly <a href="#may:first-target-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:first-target-list">[may:first-target-list]</a>.

For $n=5$, the mandatory intervals are $[0,4)$ and $[1,4)$. The remaining start-zero interval is $[0,t)$ with $t=2$ or $3$. The only optional intervals are $[1,2),[1,3),[2,4),[3,4)$; $[2,3)$ meets neither endpoint and is forbidden. Write their inclusion indicators as $e_{12},e_{13},e_{24},e_{34}\in\{0,1\}$, and let their sum be $o$. The three required coverage equations are <a id="may:four-indicators"></a>$$e_{12}+e_{13}=o/2,\qquad
 e_{24}+e_{34}=o/2,\qquad
 e_{13}+e_{24}+[t=3]=o/2,
 \label{may:four-indicators}$$ where $o\in\{0,2,4\}$. For $t=2$ the solutions are no optional intervals, the pair $\{12,24\}$, the pair $\{13,34\}$, and all four. For $t=3$ the only solution is the pair $\{12,34\}$. This proves the exhaustive Table <a href="#may:n5-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:n5-table">2</a>, including its length-seven entry.

<div id="may:n5-table">

| $m$ | Exterior intervals (exclusive right endpoints) | Polynomial factor |
|:---:|:-----------------------------------------------|:------------------|
| $3$ | $02,04,14$                                     | $b_{3,0}^{p-2}$   |
| $5$ | $03,04,12,14,34$                               | $b_{3,0}^{p-3}$   |
| $5$ | $02,04,13,14,34$                               | $b_{3,0}^{p-3}$   |
| $5$ | $02,04,12,14,24$                               | $b_{3,0}^{p-3}$   |
| $7$ | $02,04,12,13,14,24,34$                         | $b_{3,0}^{p-4}$   |

Every initial May monomial in bidegree $(q+1,u_5+1)$. The symbol $ab$ in this table denotes the interval $[a,b)$, not a product of the letters in <a href="#may:low-alphabet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:low-alphabet">[may:low-alphabet]</a>.

</div>

For completeness, the splitting calculation is also explicit. The length-three entry has, for example, a nonzero component on $01,04,12,14$. For the three length-five entries in their displayed order, take the following four rows, ordering exterior intervals lexicographically: $$\begin{array}{c|rrr}
01,04,12,13,14,34&1&-1&0\\
02,03,12,14,24,34&1&0&1\\
02,04,12,13,24,34&0&1&1\\
02,04,12,14,23,34&-1&-1&1
\end{array}$$ The common polynomial factor is $b_{3,0}^{p-3}$. The minor using the first, second, and fourth rows is $3$, which is nonzero in $\mathbb F_{1009}$. Thus these three images are linearly independent. Finally, <a href="#may:small-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:small-splitting">[may:small-splitting]</a> shows that $acb$ has a nonzero splitting differential and that $acybz$ is closed: every term in the latter differential repeats an exterior factor. This finishes the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-may-injectivity"></a>
<div id="may:injectivity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (An injective multiplication). *Multiplication by the primitive class $h_{1,4}$ is injective on actual cobar cohomology in bidegree $(q+1,u_4+1)$: $$H^{q+1,u_4+1}(P)\xrightarrow{\ h_{1,4}\ }
 H^{q+2,u_5+1}(P).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-injectivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-injectivity</summary>

*Proof.* Use the filtration in Lemma <a href="#may:page" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:page">10</a> with the explicit choice $M=3p$. If the source cohomology is nonzero, any nonzero class has leading detector a nonzero scalar multiple of $acybz B^{p-3}$, by Lemma <a href="#may:first-list" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:first-list">16</a>. Its filtration is $$F_0=3p^2-9p+13+M(2+p(p-3)).$$ The cocycle $[t_1^{p^4}]$ represents $h_{1,4}$, with degree, weight, and filtration shifts $(1,p^4,1)$. The proposed leading product therefore has filtration <a id="may:product-filtration"></a>$$F_*=3p^2-9p+14+M(2+p(p-3)).
 \label{may:product-filtration}$$

Every possible incoming May differential to this product has source cohomological degree $q+1$ and weight $u_5+1$. Table <a href="#may:n5-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:n5-table">2</a> is its complete initial-page source list. The length-three and length-five source filtrations are respectively $$5p^2-10p+15+M(2+p(p-2)),\qquad
 5p^2-15p+19+M(2+p(p-3));$$ all three length-five sources have the latter filtration. The length-three and length-five entries disappear under $d_1$, since their displayed splitting maps are injective. None can be a splitting boundary for the product itself, because its polynomial factor is a power of $B=b_{2,0}$, whereas their polynomial factor is a power of $b_{3,0}$. The length-seven source has filtration $$F_7=5p^2-20p+23+M(2+p(p-4)).$$ For $M=3p$ the difference is $$F_7-F_*=2p^2-11p+9-pM=-p^2-11p+9<0.$$ As all May differentials lower filtration, this last source cannot hit the proposed product on any later page. There are no other incoming sources.

This argument does not require $B$ to represent a permanent class by itself. Choose an actual cocycle for the given nonzero source cohomology class and multiply that entire cocycle by $[t_1^{p^4}]$. The result is an actual cocycle. Its leading associated-graded cup product is the nonzero monomial $acybz B^{p-3}h_{1,4}$, since the new exterior letter is distinct from all the old ones. An actual cocycle has no outgoing May differential; its leading detector could disappear only by becoming a boundary, which the preceding incoming-source calculation excludes at every page. Its product cohomology class is therefore nonzero. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-may-first-differential"></a>
<div id="may:first-differential" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (The first Adams–Novikov differential). *In the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for $Y$, one has $d_{q+1}(v_4)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-first-differential"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-first-differential</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#may:specialized-obstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:specialized-obstruction">[may:specialized-obstruction]</a> and Proposition <a href="#may:injectivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:injectivity">17</a> give $\sigma(d_{q+1}(v_4))=0$. Lemma <a href="#may:target-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:target-bound">15</a> identifies this specialization with the original target group. Hence $d_{q+1}(v_4)=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The remaining targets

<a id="stmt-may-remaining-table"></a>
<div id="may:remaining-table" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (All later obstruction candidates). *<span id="may:remaining-list" label="may:remaining-list"></span> For $k\geq2$ the complete initial May page in <a href="#may:obstruction-degrees" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:obstruction-degrees">[may:obstruction-degrees]</a> is Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>; all other values of $k$ give an empty page. Put $N_0=(p-1)^2$ and $N=p(p-1)-2$.*

<div id="may:remaining-table-2">

|  *$k$*  | *Initial May monomials*                                               |
|:-------:|:----------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  *$2$*  | *$xac B A^{p-2}C^{p-2}$, $\quad xacyz A^{p-2}C^{p-2}$*                |
| *$p-1$* | *$y B^3 A^{N_0-3}$, $\quad b B^2 A^{N_0-2}$, $\quad ybz B A^{N_0-2}$* |
|  *$p$*  | *$cyb A^{N+1}$, $\quad ayb B A^N$*                                    |

*Every initial May monomial for the later $v_4$ obstruction bidegrees.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-remaining-table"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-remaining-table</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#may:target-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:target-bound">15</a> allows only positions $0,1,2$ and $2\leq k\leq p+2$. Thus <a href="#may:three-position-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:three-position-weight">[may:three-position-weight]</a> applies. There are six exterior letters; $m$ is odd, so write $$m=2f+1,\quad 0\leq f\leq2,\qquad L=(p-1)k-f.$$ Reducing the weight modulo $p$ gives $H_0\equiv1+k\pmod p$, with $0\leq H_0\leq3$. The only possibilities are $k=2$ or $k=p+d$ with $-1\leq d\leq2$.

<a id="may:k2-equation"></a>
When $k=2$, $H_0=3$ forces all of $x,a,c$. The other exterior letters are an even subset of $\{y,b,z\}$. Put $T=H_2+n_B+n_C$. Substitution in <a href="#may:three-position-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:three-position-weight">[may:three-position-weight]</a> gives $$pT-n_C+H_1=p^2-p+3+f.
 \label{may:k2-equation}$$ For the four possible exterior subsets the value $e=H_1-3-f$ is $-2$ or $-1$. Therefore $n_C\equiv e\pmod p$. Since $0\leq n_C\leq L\leq2p-3$, the only possible lift is $n_C=p+e$: the lift $e$ is negative, and $2p+e\geq2p-2>L$. Equation <a href="#may:k2-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:k2-equation">[may:k2-equation]</a> now gives $T=p$. All the remaining counts follow as displayed: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c|c|c}
\text{extra exterior letters}&f&H_1&H_2&n_C&n_B&n_A\\ \hline
\varnothing&1&2&1&p-2&1&p-2\\
y,b&2&4&2&p-1&-1&p-2\\
y,z&2&3&2&p-2&0&p-2\\
b,z&2&3&3&p-2&-1&p-1
\end{array}$$ The negative counts exclude the second and fourth rows. The first and third are exactly the $k=2$ row of Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>.

<a id="may:pd-equation"></a>
<a id="may:pd-exact"></a>
Now let $k=p+d$, $-1\leq d\leq2$. Then $H_0=1+d$, and the weight equation becomes $$pT-n_C+H_1=(2-d)p+2+d+f.
 \label{may:pd-equation}$$ Since $n_C\leq T$ and $H_1\geq0$, it follows that $$(p-1)T\leq(2-d)(p-1)+(4+f).$$ Here $4+f\leq6<p-1$, so $T\leq2-d\leq3$ and $n_C\leq3$. Rearranging <a href="#may:pd-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:pd-equation">[may:pd-equation]</a> now gives $$p(T-(2-d))=2+d+f+n_C-H_1.$$ The right side lies between $-3$ and $9$, strictly between $-p$ and $p$. Both sides therefore vanish, proving the exact equations $$H_2+n_B+n_C=2-d,\qquad H_1=2+d+f+n_C.
 \label{may:pd-exact}$$

For $d\geq1$, these require $3+f\leq H_1\leq\min(2f+1,4)$, impossible for each of $f=0,1,2$. For $d=0$, exactly one of $x,a,c$ occurs, so $H_1\leq3$. Equation <a href="#may:pd-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:pd-exact">[may:pd-exact]</a> forces $f=1$, $n_C=0$, and $H_1=3$. The only exterior triples are $ayb$ and $cyb$. Their $H_2$ values are respectively $1$ and $2$, giving the two $k=p$ monomials in the table.

For $d=-1$, only $y,b,z$ can occur. If $m=1$, the choices $y,b,z$ have $(H_1,H_2)$ equal to $(1,0),(1,1),(0,1)$. Equation <a href="#may:pd-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:pd-exact">[may:pd-exact]</a> excludes $z$ and gives respectively $(n_C,n_B)=(0,3),(0,2)$ for the other two. If $m=3$, the exterior factor is $ybz$, with $f=1$, $H_1=H_2=2$; the same equations give $(n_C,n_B)=(0,1)$. Since $L=N_0-f$, these are exactly the three $k=p-1$ monomials. Every nonnegative count and every exterior subset has now been considered. ◻

</details>

The first two rows of Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> use only the first three or two coordinates, respectively. Every monomial in its last row has a large common power of $A$. The following comparisons turn these two observations into surjections on actual cobar cohomology, which are the inputs to the remaining obstruction arguments.

<a id="stmt-may-comparison"></a>
<div id="may:comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Two comparison surjections). *The following initial-page tests give surjections on actual cobar cohomology.*

1.  *If every monomial of <a href="#may:initial-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:initial-page">[may:initial-page]</a> in bidegree $(s,w)$ uses only generators with $i\leq r$, then $H^{s,w}(P_r)\longrightarrow H^{s,w}(P)$ is surjective.*

2.  *Put $A=b_{1,0}$. If every initial-page monomial in bidegree $(s,w)$ contains $A^a$, then multiplication by the actual cocycle representing $A^a$ gives a surjection $$H^{s-2a,w-pa}(P)\longrightarrow H^{s,w}(P).$$*

*These conclusions also transport through additive comparisons that identify the source and target cohomology groups and commute with the indicated maps.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-may-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-may-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* The subalgebra map includes a subset of the exterior and polynomial generators in <a href="#may:initial-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="may:initial-page">[may:initial-page]</a>; its initial-page map is therefore injective in every bidegree. Likewise, multiplication by the polynomial $A^a$ is injective in every bidegree on that page. It is induced by a filtered cochain map: $t_1$ is primitive, and <a id="may:A-cocycle"></a>$$\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}\frac{\binom pi}{p}
       [t_1^i\mid t_1^{p-i}]
 \label{may:A-cocycle}$$ is an actual cocycle, obtained by taking the integral divided reduced coproduct and then reducing modulo $p$. It has leading class $A$. For the second comparison shift the source cohomological degree, weight, and auxiliary filtration by $2a$, $pa$, and $ap(1+M)$.

Take the filtered mapping cone of either cochain map. The long exact sequence of associated-graded cohomology, together with injectivity in *all* bidegrees, identifies its initial cohomology page with the cokernel of the specified page map; there is no extra kernel from the next degree. That cokernel is zero in the stated target bidegree. Finite filtration therefore gives zero cone cohomology there. The ordinary cone long exact sequence gives the required surjection. Transport through the stated commuting comparisons preserves that surjectivity. ◻

</details>

Section <a href="#frob:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:section">6</a> removes the first two rows of Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> in actual cobar cohomology. The last row is treated in Section <a href="#top:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:section">7</a> by a homotopy argument with an explicit Adams–Novikov page bound.

<a id="frob:section"></a>
### Eliminating two obstruction degrees by Frobenius

Throughout this section $p=1009$. We eliminate the rows $k=2$ and $k=p-1$ of Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> in actual cobar cohomology. The Frobenius extension reduces this task to quotient-group cohomology with coefficients in kernel cohomology. A filtration of those coefficients then reduces the calculation to trivial-coefficient quotient groups, which we compute by May methods. We first establish these three steps and identify the coefficient modules that the two obstruction degrees require.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The Frobenius extension and its action

<a id="frob:group-law"></a>
Let $G_r$, for $r=2,3$, be the affine group scheme over $\mathbb F_p$ with coordinate algebra $$P_r=\mathbb F_p[t_1,\ldots,t_r],\qquad
 \Delta t_i=t_i\otimes1+1\otimes t_i+
       \sum_{0<l<i}t_l\otimes t_{i-l}^{p^l}.$$ The weight of $t_i$ is $u_i$. Thus, on points over a commutative $\mathbb F_p$-algebra, $$\label{frob:group-law}
 (g h)_i=g_i+h_i+\sum_{0<l<i}g_lh_{i-l}^{p^l}.$$ We write $H^a(G_r;M)$ for rational group cohomology, equivalently the cohomology of the normalized coordinate cobar complex with coefficients in the $G_r$-module $M$.

The spectral sequence below is the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence for an affine group scheme and a normal subgroup; see Jantzen (1985, pt. I, Proposition 6.6(3) and Section 6.7) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Jantzen1985](#ref-Jantzen1985)] for the spectral sequence and the quotient action on kernel cohomology. We verify the required acyclicity and compute that action for this extension.

<a id="stmt-frob-extension"></a>
<div id="frob:extension" class="lemma">

<a id="frob:exact-sequence"></a>
<a id="frob:lhss"></a>
**Lemma 21** (The Frobenius extension). *There is a finite faithfully flat exact sequence of affine group schemes $$\label{frob:exact-sequence}
 1\longrightarrow K_r=(\alpha_p)^r\longrightarrow G_r
   \xrightarrow{F} Q_r\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad O(Q_r)=\mathbb F_p[t_1^p,\ldots,t_r^p].$$ Its derived invariants give a first-quadrant spectral sequence $$\label{frob:lhss}
 {}^{\mathrm F}E_2^{a,v}
   =H^a\bigl(Q_r;H^v(K_r;\mathbb F_p)\bigr)
   \Longrightarrow H^{a+v}(G_r;\mathbb F_p).$$ It preserves weight and converges in every weight and total cohomological degree.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-extension</summary>

*Proof.* The inclusion of the Frobenius subring makes $P_r$ free of rank $p^r$, with basis $t_1^{e_1}\cdots t_r^{e_r}$, $0\leq e_i<p$. It is therefore faithfully flat. Its kernel has coordinate algebra $$O(K_r)=\mathbb F_p[e_1,\ldots,e_r]/(e_1^p,\ldots,e_r^p).$$ All the mixed terms in its coproduct vanish, so each $e_i$ is primitive. This proves the description of the kernel and the exactness of <a href="#frob:exact-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:exact-sequence">[frob:exact-sequence]</a>.

<a id="frob:regular-decomposition"></a>
Here is the acyclicity calculation needed to derive invariants. Right multiplication by a kernel point is, by <a href="#frob:group-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:group-law">[frob:group-law]</a>, simply $t_i\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}t_i+e_i$: every mixed term contains a positive $p$-power of a kernel coordinate. Hence multiplication gives an isomorphism of $K_r$-comodules $$\label{frob:regular-decomposition}
 O(Q_r)\otimes O(K_r)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }O(G_r),
 \qquad
 f\otimes e_1^{j_1}\cdots e_r^{j_r}
 \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}f t_1^{j_1}\cdots t_r^{j_r},
 \quad 0\leq j_i<p.$$ The first tensor factor has trivial $K_r$-coaction. The second is the regular comodule. In particular, $O(G_r)^{K_r}=O(Q_r)$, with its regular quotient coaction.

A cofree comodule is injective: its cofree functor is right adjoint to the exact forgetful functor to vector spaces. Equation <a href="#frob:regular-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:regular-decomposition">[frob:regular-decomposition]</a> shows that every cofree $G_r$-comodule is $K_r$-acyclic and that its $K_r$-invariants are cofree over $Q_r$. Every injective is a summand of a cofree comodule, so these assertions also hold for injectives. Finally, $M^{G_r}=(M^{K_r})^{Q_r}$. Resolving these two invariants functors successively, using the indicated cofree resolutions, gives <a href="#frob:lhss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:lhss">[frob:lhss]</a>. This argument derives $K_r$-invariants; it does not assert their exactness on arbitrary modules. The spectral sequence is first quadrant, so each fixed total cohomological degree has a finite diagonal. All coordinate weights are positive and each weight component is finite dimensional. These observations give the asserted convergence, also weight by weight. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-frob-action"></a>
<div id="frob:action" class="lemma">

<a id="frob:kernel-cohomology"></a>
<a id="frob:exterior-action"></a>
<a id="frob:polynomial-action"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Conjugation on kernel cohomology). *For $r=3$ there is an isomorphism of bigraded algebras $$\label{frob:kernel-cohomology}
 H^*(K_3;\mathbb F_p)
   =\Lambda(x,a,c)\otimes\mathbb F_p[A,B,E].$$ The exterior generators have cohomological degree one and weights $1,u_2,u_3$, and the polynomial generators have cohomological degree two and weights $p,pu_2,pu_3$, respectively. Write $\xi=t_1^p$, $\eta=t_2^p$ for quotient coordinates. With the right-conjugation convention, the quotient coaction is $$\begin{aligned}
 x&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}x,&
 a&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}a+x\xi,&
 c&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}c+x\eta+a\xi^p,\label{frob:exterior-action}\\
 A&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}A,&
 B&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}B+A\xi^p,&
 E&\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}E+A\eta^p+B\xi^{p^2}.
 \label{frob:polynomial-action}
\end{aligned}$$ Products in these formulas mean coefficient times quotient coordinate. In particular, $xac$ and $A$ are invariant. Exterior degree and polynomial degree are separately preserved. The corresponding statements for $r=2$ omit $c$ and $E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-action</summary>

*Proof.* The dual algebra of a primitive truncated coordinate $\mathbb F_p[e]/(e^p)$ is a truncated polynomial algebra after rescaling the dual basis by the invertible factorials $j!$, $j<p$. Its resolution alternates multiplication by its generator and by the $(p-1)$st power of that generator. Applying the augmentation gives one class in each cohomological degree. Its algebra of extensions is an exterior degree-one generator and a polynomial degree-two generator. The shift by two of the periodic resolution represents the latter, and its iterates are nonzero in every even degree. The former has square $[e\mid e]=\tfrac12d[e^2]$, which is zero at this odd prime. Tensoring these resolutions gives <a href="#frob:kernel-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:kernel-cohomology">[frob:kernel-cohomology]</a>, including the indicated weights.

For completeness, the naturality of the polynomial generators is Frobenius semilinear. A primitive coordinate $T$ has degree-two cocycle $$\beta(T)=\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\frac{1}{p}\binom pj
                      [T^j\mid T^{p-j}],$$ where the displayed coefficients are integers reduced modulo $p$. For a linear coordinate $T=\sum_i\lambda_i e_i$, the integral polynomial $$\frac{(\sum_i\lambda_i e_i)^p-\sum_i\lambda_i^p e_i^p}{p}$$ has reduced coproduct $\beta(\sum_i\lambda_i e_i)-\sum_i\lambda_i^p\beta(e_i)$. Thus a matrix $(\lambda_{ij})$ on degree-one coordinates induces $(\lambda_{ij}^p)$ on these degree-two cohomology classes. This is a polynomial identity in the $\lambda_{ij}$, so it also applies when they are quotient coordinate functions.

If $e$ is a kernel point, direct use of <a href="#frob:group-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:group-law">[frob:group-law]</a> gives $$(g^{-1}eg)_1=e_1,\qquad
 (g^{-1}eg)_2=e_2+e_1g_1^p,\qquad
 (g^{-1}eg)_3=e_3+e_1g_2^p+e_2g_1^{p^2}.$$ These coefficients belong to $O(Q_r)$, so the action descends to the quotient. The degree-one and degree-two naturality just proved gives <a href="#frob:exterior-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:exterior-action">[frob:exterior-action]</a> and <a href="#frob:polynomial-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:polynomial-action">[frob:polynomial-action]</a>. The exterior matrix is triangular with diagonal entries one, so its top exterior power $xac$ is fixed. The formulas also prove separate preservation of the two degrees. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Coefficient filtration and quotient cohomology

<a id="stmt-frob-coefficients"></a>
<div id="frob:coefficients" class="lemma">

<a id="frob:coefficient-page"></a>
**Lemma 23** (The coefficient-weight filtration). *Fix an exterior degree and a polynomial degree in <a href="#frob:kernel-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:kernel-cohomology">[frob:kernel-cohomology]</a>, obtaining an invariant $Q_r$-submodule $M$. At each total weight $w$, increasing coefficient weight filters the normalized quotient cobar complex $C^*(Q_r;M)_w$ by a finite filtration. Its initial cohomology page is $$\label{frob:coefficient-page}
 {}^{\mathrm C}E_1^{a,\lambda}
      =M_{\lambda}\otimes H^a(Q_r;\mathbb F_p)_{w-\lambda},$$ where $M_\lambda$ is viewed with trivial quotient action. The coefficient differential with drop $d$ has the form ${}^{\mathrm C}d_d:(a,\lambda)\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}(a+1,\lambda-d)$. If the nonidentity coefficient action has no weight drop smaller than $d$, all earlier coefficient differentials vanish, and the differential with drop $d$ is induced by that part of the coaction.*

<a id="frob:finite-D"></a>
*For the module $\operatorname{Sym}^n(A,B)$ we may write $$\label{frob:finite-D}
 A^n\langle1,D,\ldots,D^n\rangle,
 \qquad A^nD^j:=A^{n-j}B^j\quad(0\leq j\leq n).$$ This is notation for a finite-dimensional module, with action $D\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}D+\xi^p$; no inverse of $A$ is introduced.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-coefficients"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-coefficients</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#frob:exterior-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:exterior-action">[frob:exterior-action]</a> and <a href="#frob:polynomial-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:polynomial-action">[frob:polynomial-action]</a>, every nonidentity term has lower coefficient weight, its difference being the positive weight of its quotient coordinate. The cobar differential therefore preserves the increasing coefficient filtration. In the associated graded complex only the trivial coefficient action remains, giving <a href="#frob:coefficient-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-page">[frob:coefficient-page]</a>. At fixed total weight the coefficient weights lie between zero and $w$, so the filtration is finite. The stated description of its first possible differential follows by taking the leading nonzero weight-lowering component of the cobar differential. Finally, expanding $$(B+A\xi^p)^jA^{n-j}
   =\sum_{l=0}^j\binom jl A^{n-l}B^l\xi^{p(j-l)}$$ proves the stated action on <a href="#frob:finite-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:finite-D">[frob:finite-D]</a>. ◻

</details>

For either obstruction bidegree $(s,w)$, our aim is to make every group $H^a(Q_r;H^{s-a}(K_r;\mathbb F_p))_w$ zero. This makes the entire total-degree-$s$ diagonal of <a href="#frob:lhss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:lhss">[frob:lhss]</a> zero, and hence proves $H^{s,w}(G_r;\mathbb F_p)=0$. We compute these groups by the coefficient filtration <a href="#frob:coefficient-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-page">[frob:coefficient-page]</a>, using May only to bound or identify its trivial-coefficient quotient groups.

The resulting vector-space upper bound has exactly the exterior-polynomial letters used for $G_r$: kernel cohomology supplies the digit-zero letters, and the quotient’s initial May page supplies all higher digits. This description retains the kernel degree $v$. Thus Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> bounds every coefficient summand on the relevant Frobenius diagonal. Lemma <a href="#may:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:comparison">20</a> reduces $k=2$ to $G_3$ and $k=p-1$ to $G_2$. Grouping the candidates by their invariant kernel modules gives the four computations in Table <a href="#frob:coefficient-obligations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-obligations">4</a>.

For each module $M$ in that table, let $\lambda_0$ be its least coefficient weight and put <a id="frob:residual-weight"></a>$$\label{frob:residual-weight}
 \mathcal R=\frac{w-\lambda_0}{p}.$$ We call this the residual weight, and henceforth express quotient weights after division by $p$. Recall that $N_0=(p-1)^2$.

<div id="frob:coefficient-obligations">

|  $k$  | $r$ |          Kernel module $M$           | Quotient degree $a$ | $\mathcal R$ |
|:-----:|:---:|:------------------------------------:|:-------------------:|:------------:|
|  $2$  | $3$ | $xac\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}(A,B,E)$ |       $2p-4$        |   $p(p-1)$   |
|  $2$  | $3$ | $xac\operatorname{Sym}^{p-2}(A,B,E)$ |       $2p-2$        |  $p^2-p+1$   |
| $p-1$ | $2$ |   $\operatorname{Sym}^{N_0}(A,B)$    |         $1$         |    $3p+1$    |
| $p-1$ | $2$ |  $\operatorname{Sym}^{N_0-1}(A,B)$   |         $3$         |    $3p+2$    |

The coefficient-cohomology groups that must vanish. Each row means $H^a(Q_r;M)_w$, with $w=u_4+k$ in original weights.

</div>

<a id="frob:D-weight"></a>
For the first two rows, the least weights are respectively $\operatorname{wt}(xacA^{p-1})=3+p+2p^2$ and $\operatorname{wt}(xacA^{p-2})=3+2p^2$; for the last two they are $np$, where $n$ is the indicated polynomial degree. These give the displayed residual weights. Replacing an $A$ by $E$ increases the coefficient weight, after division by $p$, by $p^2+p$. This exceeds both residual weights in the $k=2$ rows, so no coefficient involving $E$ occurs in either fixed-weight complex, in any quotient degree. All four rows therefore use the finite $D$-modules of <a href="#frob:finite-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:finite-D">[frob:finite-D]</a>, multiplied by $xac$ in the first two rows. Suppressing the common invariant factor $A^n$ or $xacA^n$, a term $D^j\zeta$ requires $$\label{frob:D-weight}
 \operatorname{wt}_Q(\zeta)=\mathcal R-jp.$$ Their first possible coefficient differential lowers $j$ by one and raises the quotient degree by one: its weight drop is $p^2$ in original weights, or $p$ in these rescaled weights.

We now calculate the trivial-coefficient quotient groups needed for these four computations and their adjacent degrees. The coordinates $\xi,\eta$ have rescaled weights $1,p+1$ and <a id="frob:quotient-coproduct"></a>$$\label{frob:quotient-coproduct}
 \overline\Delta\xi=0,\qquad
 \overline\Delta\eta=\xi\otimes\xi^p.$$ In these rescaled weights, the letters $$y=h_{1,0}^{Q},\quad b=h_{2,0}^{Q},\quad
 z=h_{1,1}^{Q},\quad C=b_{1,0}^{Q}$$ have weights $1,p+1,p,p$, respectively; these are exactly the letters $y,b,z,C$ used in Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>.

<a id="stmt-frob-quotient-groups"></a>
<div id="frob:quotient-groups" class="lemma">

<a id="frob:small-monomials"></a>
**Lemma 24** (The small quotient groups). *For $Q_2$ or $Q_3$, at any positive rescaled weight $w<p^2$ all available May letters are $y,b,z,C$. Their monomials and bidegrees are $$\label{frob:small-monomials}
 C^j y^e z^f b^g,\quad
 j\geq0,\quad e,f,g\in\{0,1\},\qquad
 a=2j+e+f+g,\quad w=p(j+f+g)+e+g.$$ The following lists are exhaustive, with a dash denoting the empty list:*

<div class="center">

| *Rescaled weight* | *Cohomological degree* | *May monomials* |
|:------------------|:-----------------------|:----------------|
| *$p(p-1)$*        | *$2p-4$*               | *—*             |
| *$p(p-1)$*        | *$2p-3$*               | *$zC^{p-2}$*    |
| *$p(p-2)$*        | *$2p-4$*               | *$C^{p-2}$*     |
| *$p(p-3)$*        | *$2p-5$*               | *—*             |
| *$p+2$*           | *$1,3$*                | *—*             |
| *$p+2$*           | *$2$*                  | *$yb$*          |
| *$2p+2$*          | *$2$*                  | *—*             |
| *$2p+2$*          | *$3$*                  | *$ybz$*         |
| *$3p+2$*          | *$3$*                  | *—*             |
| *$2$*             | *$1,3$*                | *—*             |

</div>

*The classes $C^{p-2}$ and $zC^{p-2}$ in the table are nonzero in actual quotient cohomology. There is also a nonzero class $\upsilon$ of degree two and weight $p+2$, detected by $yb$, and $\upsilon z$ is nonzero in degree three and weight $2p+2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-quotient-groups"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-quotient-groups</summary>

*Proof.* The next exterior weights are $p^2$ and $p^2+p$; the third-coordinate weight, if present, is $p^2+p+1$. The next polynomial weight is $p^2$. All are outside the asserted range. Lemma <a href="#may:page" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:page">10</a> therefore gives exactly <a href="#frob:small-monomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:small-monomials">[frob:small-monomials]</a>. To verify the table, first reduce its weight equation modulo $p$: its remainder is $e+g\in\{0,1,2\}$. For weights divisible by $p$ it forces $e=g=0$, after which the degree equation fixes $j$ and $f$. For the listed weights congruent to two it forces $e=g=1$; substitution into both equations gives the entries shown. The weight-two entries follow in the same way, since $j=f=g=0$ at that weight. All weights in the table are within the asserted range, because $3p+2<p(p-1)$ at $p=1009$.

The cocycles $z=[\xi^p]$ and $C=\beta(\xi)$ come from the primitive coordinate $\xi$. Their product $zC^{p-2}$ is an actual cocycle with the indicated nonzero leading May monomial. The preceding cohomological degree at its weight has no May monomial in any filtration. It consequently cannot become a boundary on any May page. An actual cocycle has no outgoing May differential, proving its nonvanishing. The factor $C^{p-2}$ is therefore nonzero as well.

For the other two classes, use the cobar convention $d[u\mid v]=d[u]\mid v-u\mid d[v]$ with $d[\eta]=[\xi\mid\xi^p]$. The corrected cocycle <a id="frob:corrected-cocycle"></a>$$\label{frob:corrected-cocycle}
 U=[\xi\mid\eta]+\frac12[\xi^2\mid\xi^p]$$ is closed: its two differentials are $-[\xi\mid\xi\mid\xi^p]$ and $[\xi\mid\xi\mid\xi^p]$, respectively. In the ordinary May filtration its first summand has degree four and the correction has degree three. Its leading detector is thus $yb$. The table excludes both an incoming and an outgoing May differential, so $\upsilon=[U]\neq0$. Similarly $$U\mid\xi^p
   =[\xi\mid\eta\mid\xi^p]
       +\frac12[\xi^2\mid\xi^p\mid\xi^p]$$ is a closed representative with leading detector $ybz$. Its correction has smaller May filtration. The absence of a degree-two May monomial at weight $2p+2$ excludes every possible incoming differential, and the explicit cocycle excludes outgoing differentials. Hence $\upsilon z\neq0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The row $k=2$

<a id="stmt-frob-k2"></a>
<div id="frob:k2" class="proposition">

**Proposition 25** (The row $k=2$). *At $s=4p-3$ and $w=u_4+2$, the cobar cohomology group in Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-k2"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-k2</summary>

*Proof.* We prove vanishing for $G_3$ by treating the first two rows of Table <a href="#frob:coefficient-obligations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-obligations">4</a>. Their kernel degrees are $2p+1$ and $2p-1$, so their coefficient differentials cannot mix.

<a id="frob:k2-differential"></a>
For $M=xac\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}(A,B,E)$, we have $\mathcal R=p(p-1)$ and quotient degree $a=2p-4$. After the weight exclusion of $E$, the finite module is $xacA^{p-1}\langle1,D,\ldots,D^{p-1}\rangle$. The sole candidate from Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> is $D C^{p-2}$, suppressing the invariant factor $xacA^{p-1}$. Its quotient weight is $p(p-2)$ by <a href="#frob:D-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:D-weight">[frob:D-weight]</a>. The translation of $D$ gives, on the first possible coefficient page, $$\label{frob:k2-differential}
 D[C^{p-2}]\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}\pm[zC^{p-2}].$$ The target has quotient degree $2p-3$ and weight $p(p-1)$. Both source and target are nonzero actual quotient-cohomology classes by Lemma <a href="#frob:quotient-groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:quotient-groups">24</a>; they are present on this page because all earlier coefficient differentials vanish. Thus <a href="#frob:k2-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:k2-differential">[frob:k2-differential]</a> removes the candidate.

We record the adjacent-degree checks. A differential on this page entering its source would have $D$-degree two, quotient degree $2p-5$, and quotient weight $p(p-3)$; this group is zero by Lemma <a href="#frob:quotient-groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:quotient-groups">24</a>. The target, having $D$-degree zero, has no outgoing coefficient differential. Its first-page incoming source can only have $D$-degree one, and <a href="#frob:small-monomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:small-monomials">[frob:small-monomials]</a> in degree $2p-4$, weight $p(p-2)$, gives only $C^{p-2}$. Thus no other source can cancel the nonzero map in <a href="#frob:k2-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:k2-differential">[frob:k2-differential]</a>. In the original total degree, the same equation, or the exhaustive candidate table, leaves no other coefficient-leading term in this vertical summand. Hence a change of representative cannot replace the removed class.

Now take the full second summand $M=xac\operatorname{Sym}^{p-2}(A,B,E)$, with $\mathcal R=p^2-p+1$ and quotient degree $2p-2$. Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> leaves only its $D$-degree-zero candidate, whose coefficient $xacA^{p-2}$ is invariant. Its quotient detector $yzC^{p-2}$ is already a splitting boundary, since $db=yz$ and $dC=0$ on the May page. This also has a direct cobar realization: $yz=d[\eta]$ and $C$ is an actual cocycle, so $yzC^{p-2}$ is exact. It is the only May monomial in this quotient bidegree, as follows by putting $a=2p-2$, $w=p^2-p+1$ in <a href="#frob:small-monomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:small-monomials">[frob:small-monomials]</a>. It therefore contributes no actual quotient cohomology in <a href="#frob:coefficient-page" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-page">[frob:coefficient-page]</a>. These are all initial candidates of total degree $4p-3$ and weight $u_4+2$. The coefficient spectral sequences consequently vanish there, so all corresponding terms of <a href="#frob:lhss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:lhss">[frob:lhss]</a> are zero. Its convergence proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The row $k=p-1$

<a id="stmt-frob-kpminus1"></a>
<div id="frob:kpminus1" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26** (The row $k=p-1$). *Put $N_0=(p-1)^2$. At $s=2N_0+1$ and $w=u_4+p-1$, the cobar cohomology group in Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a> is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-kpminus1"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-kpminus1</summary>

*Proof.* We treat the last two rows of Table <a href="#frob:coefficient-obligations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:coefficient-obligations">4</a> for $G_2$, using the finite module <a href="#frob:finite-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:finite-D">[frob:finite-D]</a> in each case. For $n=N_0$, the residual weight is $\mathcal R=3p+1$ and the quotient degree is one. We show directly that every one-cocycle vanishes. The leading candidates could survive only if lower powers of $D$ supplied corrections. The crossed-cocycle equation will rule these out: it requires unequal mixed coefficients where every available correction has equal ones.

<a id="frob:crossed-equation"></a>
<a id="frob:crossed-coefficients"></a>
<a id="frob:asymmetric-pair"></a>
Write $\xi(g)=r$, $\eta(g)=u$, $\xi(h)=s$, $\eta(h)=v$, so that $$\xi(gh)=r+s,\qquad\eta(gh)=u+v+rs^p.$$ A normalized one-cocycle is a polynomial $f(D;g)$ satisfying $$\label{frob:crossed-equation}
 f(D;gh)=f(D;g)+f(D+r^p;h).$$ Since $D$ has weight $p$ and $4p>3p+1$, it must have the form $$f(D;g)=D^3f_3(g)+D^2f_2(g)+Df_1(g)+f_0(g).$$ All these powers are in the finite module, since $N_0\geq3$. For a polynomial function $\varphi$ write $\delta\varphi(g,h)=\varphi(gh)-\varphi(g)-\varphi(h)$. Comparing the four coefficients in <a href="#frob:crossed-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:crossed-equation">[frob:crossed-equation]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \delta f_3&=0,\nonumber\\
 \delta f_2&=3r^pf_3(h),\nonumber\\
 \delta f_1&=2r^pf_2(h)+3r^{2p}f_3(h),
       \label{frob:crossed-coefficients}\\
 \delta f_0&=r^pf_1(h)+r^{2p}f_2(h)+r^{3p}f_3(h).
       \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Homogeneity forces $$\begin{aligned}
 f_3&=a_3\xi,\\
 f_2&=b_2\eta+c_2\xi^{p+1},\\
 f_1&=d_1\eta\xi^p+e_1\xi^{2p+1},\\
 f_0&=r_0\xi^{3p+1}+s_0\eta\xi^{2p}
                       +t_0\eta^2\xi^{p-1}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the coefficients $a_3,b_2,c_2,d_1,e_1,r_0,s_0,t_0$ lie in $\mathbb F_p$. The second equation of <a href="#frob:crossed-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:crossed-coefficients">[frob:crossed-coefficients]</a>, together with $$\delta\eta=rs^p,\qquad
 \delta(\xi^{p+1})=r^ps+rs^p,$$ forces $c_2=3a_3$ and $b_2=-3a_3$. The next equation has coefficient $2b_2$ on $r^pv$ and zero on $us^p$. On the other hand, $$\label{frob:asymmetric-pair}
 \delta(\eta\xi^p)
       =us^p+vr^p+r^{p+1}s^p+rs^{2p},$$ and $\delta(\xi^{2p+1})$ contains neither $u$ nor $v$. Its left side must therefore have equal coefficients on $r^pv$ and $us^p$. Since $2b_2=-6a_3$ and $6\neq0$ in $\mathbb F_{1009}$, we obtain $a_3=0$, and then $b_2=c_2=0$. No term in $f_0$ can alter this coefficient-of-$D$ equation.

Now $f_1$ is primitive. Equation <a href="#frob:asymmetric-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:asymmetric-pair">[frob:asymmetric-pair]</a> first forces $d_1=0$, and the coefficient of $r^{2p}s$ in $\delta(\xi^{2p+1})$ then forces $e_1=0$. Finally $f_0$ is primitive. In its reduced coproduct the monomial $u^2s^{p-1}$ has coefficient $t_0$, so $t_0=0$. The monomial $us^{2p}$ then has coefficient $s_0$, so $s_0=0$. The coefficient of $r^{3p}s$ in $\delta(\xi^{3p+1})$ is one, giving $r_0=0$. Every normalized cocycle is thus zero, proving the required $H^1$ vanishing, including all lower-$D$ corrections.

<a id="frob:h3-differential"></a>
For $n=N_0-1$, the residual weight is $\mathcal R=3p+2$ and the desired quotient degree is three. The first coefficient differential comes from the term $2D\xi^p$ in $$(D+\xi^p)^2-D^2=2D\xi^p+\xi^{2p}.$$ Using the actual class $\upsilon$ of Lemma <a href="#frob:quotient-groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:quotient-groups">24</a>, it is $$\label{frob:h3-differential}
 D^2\upsilon\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}\pm2D\upsilon z.$$ The coefficient differential first prepends $z$. Its product $z\upsilon$ agrees with $\upsilon z$: both are actual cocycles with the same leading May monomial $ybz$, with coefficient $+1$ after two exterior interchanges, and the list in Equation <a href="#frob:small-monomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:small-monomials">[frob:small-monomials]</a> leaves no other initial May monomial or lower-filtration cohomology in this bidegree. Thus the product order in Equation <a href="#frob:h3-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:h3-differential">[frob:h3-differential]</a> is justified directly. Both powers of $D$ lie in the finite module, since $n\geq3$. Equation <a href="#frob:D-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:D-weight">[frob:D-weight]</a> gives quotient degree and weight $(2,p+2)$ for the source and $(3,2p+2)$ for the target. Both therefore have the required residual weight $3p+2$. All earlier coefficient differentials vanish, so source and target are still their actual quotient-cohomology classes on this first possible page. They are both nonzero by Lemma <a href="#frob:quotient-groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:quotient-groups">24</a>.

A same-page incoming differential to the source would require $D$-degree three and quotient $H^1$ of weight two, which is zero. The only same-page incoming source for the target is $D^2H^2(Q_2)_{p+2}$, the one-dimensional span displayed in <a href="#frob:h3-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:h3-differential">[frob:h3-differential]</a>. A same-page outgoing differential from the target is proportional to $\upsilon z^2=0$. Indeed $[\xi^p\mid\xi^p]=\tfrac12d[\xi^{2p}]$, so this last vanishing also follows directly in the cobar complex. The term $\xi^{2p}$ in the expansion of $D^2$ lowers two $D$-levels and cannot cancel its first differential. Since $2\neq0$ in $\mathbb F_p$, <a href="#frob:h3-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:h3-differential">[frob:h3-differential]</a> removes the target.

There is no other degree-three coefficient-leading term. In fact, the possibilities for $D$-degrees $0,1,2,3$ require quotient degree three at respective weights $3p+2,2p+2,p+2,2$; by Lemma <a href="#frob:quotient-groups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:quotient-groups">24</a>, only the second has a candidate, namely $ybz$. Larger $D$-degrees exceed the residual weight. Thus there is no replacement in a lower coefficient filtration. The initial candidate table excludes every other kernel degree or exterior summand at this total bidegree. Both possible terms of <a href="#frob:lhss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="frob:lhss">[frob:lhss]</a> consequently vanish, and the proposition follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-frob-vanishing"></a>
<div id="frob:vanishing" class="corollary">

**Corollary 27**. *The obstruction groups in bidegrees $$(s,w)=(2q+1,u_4+2),\qquad
 (s,w)=(q(p-1)+1,u_4+p-1)$$ vanish in the additive cobar calculation of Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>. Among its rows with $k\geq2$, only the row $k=p$ remains to be addressed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-frob-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-frob-vanishing</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Propositions <a href="#frob:k2" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:k2">25</a> and <a href="#frob:kpminus1" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frob:kpminus1">26</a>, and the exhaustive list in Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="top:section"></a>
### The final obstruction and the finite cofiber

We retain the prime $p=1009$ and put <a id="top:constants"></a>$$\label{top:constants}
 N=p(p-1)-2,\qquad K=p(p-2)+1=(p-1)^2,
 \qquad W=2p^2+4p+1.$$ The spectrum $Y$ of Proposition <a href="#low:V3" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="low:V3">13</a> has $BP_*Y=R/I_4$ and a binary two-sided unital multiplication $\mu:Y\wedge Y\longrightarrow Y$ with unit $\eta:S^0_{(p)}\to Y$. All spheres and smash products in this section are $p$-local. We shall use the product and convergence assertions of Proposition <a href="#fw:anss-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:anss-pairing">7</a> and Proposition <a href="#fw:anss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:anss">6</a>. In this section the second superscript of $E_r^{s,w}$ is weight: it denotes the group of internal degree $qw$ in the earlier notation.

<a id="top:An-surjection"></a>
The May comparison and the remaining-target calculation, Lemma <a href="#may:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:comparison">20</a> and Table <a href="#may:remaining-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:remaining-table">19</a>, give a surjection $$\label{top:An-surjection}
 A^N\colon E_2^{5,W}(Y)\longrightarrow
 E_2^{qp+1,u_4+p}(Y).$$ Indeed, every initial May monomial in the target is divisible by $A^N$, and the two degree identities are $$5+2N=qp+1,\qquad W+pN=u_4+p.$$ For the source, $W<u_4$, so specialization to the additive Hopf algebra identifies the normalized cobar groups in all three degrees $4,5,6$. For the target, $u_4+p<u_5$; a positive $v_4$ coefficient would leave weight $p$, less than the shortest adjacent tensor length $qp=(qp+1)-1$. Thus the target groups in degrees $qp,qp+1,qp+2$ also agree with their additive specializations, by Lemma <a href="#fw:normalized-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fw:normalized-bound">4</a>. The two triples differ by $2N$. Cup multiplication by the actual carry cocycle representing $A^N$ commutes with specialization, so the filtered surjection of Lemma <a href="#may:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="may:comparison">20</a> transports through this commuting square to the displayed surjection for $Y$.

<a id="top:lift-criterion"></a>
Let $\alpha\in\pi_{q-1}S^0$ and $\beta\in\pi_{pq-2}S^0$ be the sphere classes constructed in Lemma <a href="#top:sphere-classes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="top:sphere-classes">29</a>, detected by $[t_1]$ and $A$, respectively, and write $d=|\beta|=pq-2$. The two-cell spectrum that we use for lifts is <a id="top:J-definition"></a>$$\label{top:J-definition}
 J=\Sigma^{-q}C(\alpha),\qquad
 \varepsilon:J\longrightarrow S^0,$$ where $C(\alpha)$ is the cofiber of $\alpha:S^{q-1}\to S^0$ and $\varepsilon$ is its desuspended top quotient. Thus $J$ has cells in degrees $-q$ and zero.
